People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. [Name], it's... it's a lot of things, really, but I guess the main thing that finally made me make the appointment is my eating. It’s just gotten completely off track lately. I mean, some days I wake up and the thought of food is just... I don't know, it's almost exhausting. I’ll barely get down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before I head in for a shift. And working in healthcare support, you know, you're on your feet, you're moving patients, you're running around, so you need the energy. But I just can't seem to force myself to eat a real meal. And I’ve really started to notice it, too. Like, my scrubs, the ones I've worn for a couple of years now, they’re just hanging loose on me. I have to pull the drawstrings so tight just to keep the pants up. And it's not like I'm trying to lose weight or go on a diet or anything like that. I just... I don't have the appetite, and the weight is just falling off me without me even trying. It's been going on for a while now, and I just keep thinking it'll get better, that I'll wake up hungry one day, but it hasn't happened.

Yeah... yeah, I have. I don't even know if "sad" is the right word for it, really. It's more like... well, you know how I mentioned my mornings before, with the coffee and everything? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I check my temperature sometimes at work, you know, just out of habit because we have the thermometers right there at the nurses' station, the temporal ones that just scan across the forehead. And it's always normal, always right around 98.6 or maybe 97.9, depending on the time of day. I haven't had any fevers at all. I've always liked the weight of it, it helps me feel grounded, I guess. But I'm not waking up drenched or anything where I have to change my sheets. It's not like that. And as for lumps or swelling, no, I haven't noticed anything like that either. I mean, I shower every day, obviously, and I feel like I'd notice if there was something new or weird going on in my neck or under my arms. You hear stories, working in the hospital, you see patients come in with these swollen lymph nodes and it turns out to be something awful, so I'm pretty hyper-aware of my own body in that regard. But no, nothing like that. It's just... it's just the weight loss and this heavy feeling I was talking about. I keep checking my neck when I shave, just to be sure, but it's all completely normal.

Happy? Oh, no, definitely not happy. Honestly, it's... it's actually really unsettling. Like I was saying before, I'm not on a diet or anything. I know a lot of the folks I work with, especially some of the nurses on the floor, they're always talking about intermittent fasting or doing keto or whatever the new trend is, and they get so thrilled when they drop a few pounds. But for me, it just feels wrong. It really worries me, actually. 

I put on my jeans the other day—just my regular everyday Levi's that I always wear on my days off when I'm just running to the grocery store or the pharmacy—and I had to notch my belt two whole holes tighter. Two holes! And even then, the denim just looked all baggy and... I don't know, just sad on me. It really freaked me out for a minute. It worries me because it feels like my body is just shrinking, and I'm not doing anything to cause it on purpose, other than just not being able to stomach the thought of eating. It feels like my body is kind of failing me, or maybe I'm failing it, but I just don't have the energy to fix it. 

So yeah, I'm definitely not relieved. If anything, it just adds to that whole heavy, dragging feeling I told you about, because now I'm lying there in bed staring at the ceiling, thinking about how my clothes don't fit and how I'm just sort of wasting away, and I still can't bring myself to even make a piece of toast. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. When I'm helping transfer a patient from the bed to the chair, or adjusting their IV lines—not that I start the IVs, but I help make sure the tubing isn't tangled when we move them—I need to have a good grip. If my hands were shaking, I think I'd notice, and the nurses would definitely notice. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. 

And my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. If anything, it feels like everything in me is just moving in slow motion these days. Like I said about that heavy feeling in the mornings, it's almost like my heart is just barely thudding along, just doing the absolute bare minimum to keep me going. I don't get those sudden bursts where it feels like it's beating out of my chest. I know what that feels like—I had a panic attack once, years ago, back when I was still in school for my associate's degree, right before finals. My heart was going a mile a minute then, but I haven't had anything like that recently.

As for feeling hot... well, I mentioned the heavy comforter earlier, right? I'm usually the one who's a little chilly, if anything. The hospital is always kept freezing cold, especially on the surgical floors, so I'm used to wearing my long-sleeve undershirts beneath my scrubs. Even when I'm running around, doing a million things, answering call bells, I don't really break a sweat or feel overheated. I definitely don't feel intolerant to the heat. Honestly, a warm room sounds kind of nice right now, compared to how cold and empty everything else feels. So, no, no tremors, no racing heart, and definitely not feeling too hot. Just... just the same slow, heavy drag.

healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Besides the appetite and mood changes, have you noticed any unexplained fevers, night sweats, or new lumps or swelling anywhere, like in your neck, armpits, or groin?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I check my temperature sometimes at work, you know, just out of habit because we have the thermometers right there at the nurses' station, the temporal ones that just scan across the forehead. I know a lot of the folks I work with, especially some of the nurses on the floor, they're always talking about intermittent fasting or doing keto or whatever the new trend is, and they get so thrilled when they drop a few pounds. But for me, it just feels wrong. It really worries me, actually. \n\nI put on my jeans the other day—just my regular everyday Levi's that I always wear on my days off when I'm just running to the grocery store or the pharmacy—and I had to notch my belt two whole holes tighter. It worries me because it feels like my body is just shrinking, and I'm not doing anything to cause it on purpose, other than just not being able to stomach the thought of eating. It feels like my body is kind of failing me, or maybe I'm failing it, but I just don't have the energy to fix it. \n\nSo yeah, I'm definitely not relieved. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors in your hands, a racing or pounding heartbeat, or feeling unusually hot or intolerant of warm environments recently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. When I'm helping transfer a patient from the bed to the chair, or adjusting their IV lines—not that I start the IVs, but I help make sure the tubing isn't tangled when we move them—I need to have a good grip. If my hands were shaking, I think I'd notice, and the nurses would definitely notice. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. \n\nAnd my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. If anything, it feels like everything in me is just moving in slow motion these days. Like I said about that heavy feeling in the mornings, it's almost like my heart is just barely thudding along, just doing the absolute bare minimum to keep me going. I don't get those sudden bursts where it feels like it's beating out of my chest. I know what that feels like—I had a panic attack once, years ago, back when I was still in school for my associate's degree, right before finals. My heart was going a mile a minute then, but I haven't had anything like that recently.\n\nAs for feeling hot... well, I mentioned the heavy comforter earlier, right? I'm usually the one who's a little chilly, if anything. The hospital is always kept freezing cold, especially on the surgical floors, so I'm used to wearing my long-sleeve undershirts beneath my scrubs. Even when I'm running around, doing a million things, answering call bells, I don't really break a sweat or feel overheated. I definitely don't feel intolerant to the heat. Honestly, a warm room sounds kind of nice right now, compared to how cold and empty everything else feels. So, no, no tremors, no racing heart, and definitely not feeling too hot. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hurting myself? No, no, I haven't made any kind of plan to do anything like that. I mean, working at the hospital, sometimes I get pulled from my regular duties to act as a 1:1 sitter for patients who are on suicide watch. I sit in the room with them for my whole twelve-hour shift, just watching them, making sure they're safe. I see what that kind of thing does to them, and I see the families when they come to visit, so I know the reality of it. Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. 

But... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. 

It just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.

Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. \n\nBut... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. \n\nIt just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Depressive Episode Duration\nmanifestation: \"The steady depression and loss of interest settled in later, and it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? Like maybe I just caught a bug going around the hospital, or I was just stressed about some new protocol they rolled out on the floor. But no, it's been way, way longer than that. 

I actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. They made all the support staff park in the satellite lot, the one that's a good four blocks away, and we had to start taking this little shuttle bus in. 

I remember sitting on that shuttle bus one morning, looking out the window at the rain—it was a Tuesday, I think, and it was pouring—and just feeling this absolute, crushing weight drop onto me. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. 

Before that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? That really locked in about a year and four months ago. 

Since then, it's just been every single day. Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.

I mean, I shower every day, obviously, and I feel like I'd notice if there was something new or weird going on in my neck or under my arms. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors in your hands, a racing or pounding heartbeat, or feeling unusually hot or intolerant of warm environments recently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. \n\nAnd my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. 

See, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. 

And then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. 

And you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. 

So I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. Sometimes I'll just go into the clean supply closet and stand there staring at the stacks of extra blankets, just trying to catch my breath, because fighting the heavy feeling and the voices and the tracking all at once... it's just too much.
=====================================

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], it's... it's a lot of things, really, but I guess the main thing that finally made me make the appointment is my eating. It’s just gotten completely off track lately. I mean, some days I wake up and the thought of food is just... I don't know, it's almost exhausting. I’ll barely get down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before I head in for a shift. And working in healthcare support, you know, you're on your feet, you're moving patients, you're running around, so you need the energy. But I just can't seem to force myself to eat a real meal. And I’ve really started to notice it, too. Like, my scrubs, the ones I've worn for a couple of years now, they’re just hanging loose on me. I have to pull the drawstrings so tight just to keep the pants up. And it's not like I'm trying to lose weight or go on a diet or anything like that. I just... I don't have the appetite, and the weight is just falling off me without me even trying. It's been going on for a while now, and I just keep thinking it'll get better, that I'll wake up hungry one day, but it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a change in your mood, like feeling sad, down, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I don't even know if \"sad\" is the right word for it, really. It's more like... well, you know how I mentioned my mornings before, with the coffee and everything? I've been trying to figure out how to explain it to myself. Most mornings, my alarm goes off at 4:45 AM, because I usually like to have a little time to myself before the shift starts, just to ease into things. But lately, I wake up and I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling. \n\nI wake up with this... this incredibly heavy, almost hollow feeling right in the center of my chest. It's like the entire day is already completely ruined before I even get out from under the covers, you know? Before I even put on my hospital badge. I'll just look over at my lanyard sitting on the nightstand, and the thought of clipping it on and walking through those doors just feels impossible. \n\nI used to actually enjoy my morning routine—I'd watch the local news, check the weather, maybe read a bit on my phone. But now, all of that just feels so pointless. I have zero interest in the news or anything else I used to like. I just feel this dragging, empty weight, and it takes every single ounce of willpower I have just to put my feet on the floor. It's just this dark, hollow cloud that sits on me from the second I open my eyes, and I just keep wondering how I'm supposed to get through another twelve-hour shift feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Besides the appetite and mood changes, have you noticed any unexplained fevers, night sweats, or new lumps or swelling anywhere, like in your neck, armpits, or groin?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. And it's always normal, always right around 98.6 or maybe 97.9, depending on the time of day. I haven't had any fevers at all. And night sweats... well, sometimes I wake up feeling a little warm, but I think that's just because I sleep with a heavy comforter. I've always liked the weight of it, it helps me feel grounded, I guess. But I'm not waking up drenched or anything where I have to change my sheets. It's not like that. And as for lumps or swelling, no, I haven't noticed anything like that either. I mean, I shower every day, obviously, and I feel like I'd notice if there was something new or weird going on in my neck or under my arms. You hear stories, working in the hospital, you see patients come in with these swollen lymph nodes and it turns out to be something awful, so I'm pretty hyper-aware of my own body in that regard. But no, nothing like that. It's just... it's just the weight loss and this heavy feeling I was talking about. I keep checking my neck when I shave, just to be sure, but it's all completely normal.\n\nDoctor: When you notice your clothes fitting looser, how do you feel about that weight loss—are you happy or relieved about it, or does it worry you?\n\nPatient: Happy? Oh, no, definitely not happy. Honestly, it's... it's actually really unsettling. Like I was saying before, I'm not on a diet or anything. I know a lot of the folks I work with, especially some of the nurses on the floor, they're always talking about intermittent fasting or doing keto or whatever the new trend is, and they get so thrilled when they drop a few pounds. But for me, it just feels wrong. It really worries me, actually. \n\nI put on my jeans the other day—just my regular everyday Levi's that I always wear on my days off when I'm just running to the grocery store or the pharmacy—and I had to notch my belt two whole holes tighter. Two holes! And even then, the denim just looked all baggy and... I don't know, just sad on me. It really freaked me out for a minute. It worries me because it feels like my body is just shrinking, and I'm not doing anything to cause it on purpose, other than just not being able to stomach the thought of eating. It feels like my body is kind of failing me, or maybe I'm failing it, but I just don't have the energy to fix it. \n\nSo yeah, I'm definitely not relieved. If anything, it just adds to that whole heavy, dragging feeling I told you about, because now I'm lying there in bed staring at the ceiling, thinking about how my clothes don't fit and how I'm just sort of wasting away, and I still can't bring myself to even make a piece of toast. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors in your hands, a racing or pounding heartbeat, or feeling unusually hot or intolerant of warm environments recently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. When I'm helping transfer a patient from the bed to the chair, or adjusting their IV lines—not that I start the IVs, but I help make sure the tubing isn't tangled when we move them—I need to have a good grip. If my hands were shaking, I think I'd notice, and the nurses would definitely notice. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. \n\nAnd my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. If anything, it feels like everything in me is just moving in slow motion these days. Like I said about that heavy feeling in the mornings, it's almost like my heart is just barely thudding along, just doing the absolute bare minimum to keep me going. I don't get those sudden bursts where it feels like it's beating out of my chest. I know what that feels like—I had a panic attack once, years ago, back when I was still in school for my associate's degree, right before finals. My heart was going a mile a minute then, but I haven't had anything like that recently.\n\nAs for feeling hot... well, I mentioned the heavy comforter earlier, right? I'm usually the one who's a little chilly, if anything. The hospital is always kept freezing cold, especially on the surgical floors, so I'm used to wearing my long-sleeve undershirts beneath my scrubs. Even when I'm running around, doing a million things, answering call bells, I don't really break a sweat or feel overheated. I definitely don't feel intolerant to the heat. Honestly, a warm room sounds kind of nice right now, compared to how cold and empty everything else feels. So, no, no tremors, no racing heart, and definitely not feeling too hot. Just... just the same slow, heavy drag.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? No, no, I haven't made any kind of plan to do anything like that. I mean, working at the hospital, sometimes I get pulled from my regular duties to act as a 1:1 sitter for patients who are on suicide watch. I sit in the room with them for my whole twelve-hour shift, just watching them, making sure they're safe. I see what that kind of thing does to them, and I see the families when they come to visit, so I know the reality of it. Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. \n\nBut... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. \n\nIt just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: How long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? Like maybe I just caught a bug going around the hospital, or I was just stressed about some new protocol they rolled out on the floor. But no, it's been way, way longer than that. \n\nI actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. They made all the support staff park in the satellite lot, the one that's a good four blocks away, and we had to start taking this little shuttle bus in. \n\nI remember sitting on that shuttle bus one morning, looking out the window at the rain—it was a Tuesday, I think, and it was pouring—and just feeling this absolute, crushing weight drop onto me. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. \n\nBefore that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? That really locked in about a year and four months ago. \n\nSince then, it's just been every single day. Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things that others don't see, or having unusual beliefs—like feeling you're being punished or that something terrible is happening to your body—during this time?\n\nPatient: Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. \n\nSee, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. \n\nAnd then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. \n\nAnd you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. \n\nSo I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. Sometimes I'll just go into the clean supply closet and stand there staring at the stacks of extra blankets, just trying to catch my breath, because fighting the heavy feeling and the voices and the tracking all at once... it's just too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

This rules out non-psychotic F32.2/F32.9 and raises F32.3 (severe depressive episode with psychotic symptoms) versus F25.1 (schizoaffective disorder, depressive type) if psychotic symptoms persist independent of mood severity.", "is_final": false}

psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the sense that people were sending signs through the schedule manifestation: The steady depression and loss of interest settled in later, and it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? No, no, I haven't made any kind of plan to do anything like that. I mean, working at the hospital, sometimes I get pulled from my regular duties to act as a 1:1 sitter for patients who are on suicide watch. I sit in the room with them for my whole twelve-hour shift, just watching them, making sure they're safe. I see what that kind of thing does to them, and I see the families when they come to visit, so I know the reality of it. Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. \n\nBut... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. \n\nIt just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: How long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? Like maybe I just caught a bug going around the hospital, or I was just stressed about some new protocol they rolled out on the floor. But no, it's been way, way longer than that. \n\nI actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. They made all the support staff park in the satellite lot, the one that's a good four blocks away, and we had to start taking this little shuttle bus in. \n\nI remember sitting on that shuttle bus one morning, looking out the window at the rain—it was a Tuesday, I think, and it was pouring—and just feeling this absolute, crushing weight drop onto me. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. \n\nBefore that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? That really locked in about a year and four months ago. \n\nSince then, it's just been every single day. Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things that others don't see, or having unusual beliefs—like feeling you're being punished or that something terrible is happening to your body—during this time?\n\nPatient: Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. \n\nSee, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. \n\nAnd then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. \n\nAnd you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. \n\nSo I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. Sometimes I'll just go into the clean supply closet and stand there staring at the stacks of extra blankets, just trying to catch my breath, because fighting the heavy feeling and the voices and the tracking all at once... it's just too much.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave there been times when you were not feeling that heavy, depressed mood at all, but you still heard the voices or felt that people were tracking your charting?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, actually, there was a time like that. It’s funny you ask, because I remember thinking it was so strange. There was this period when that heavy, wet cement feeling I was telling you about actually eased up. It lifted enough that I felt almost like my old self. I even started running a little again. I used to jog around my neighborhood, just a couple of miles to clear my head, and I had completely stopped doing that when the hollow feeling set in. But for a little bit there, I was actually lacing up my sneakers, getting outside, and just getting through the day without feeling like I was dragging a thousand pounds behind me. I’d wake up and the alarm wouldn’t feel like a punishment. 

But the weird thing is, the other stuff didn't stop. You'd think if my mood was better, my head would clear out entirely, right? But it didn't. For several weeks, even while I was feeling physically okay and getting through my shifts just fine, I was still dealing with it. I’d be walking down to the basement level to grab a bariatric wheelchair or something—totally empty hallway, nobody around at all, just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights—and I’d still hear those comments. Just whispering right there, criticizing me. 

And it wasn't just the voices, either. We have this big printed schedule they pin up on the corkboard in the breakroom, right by the time clock. Even when I was feeling good and running and eating normally, I kept reading that schedule over and over like it had hidden warnings about me. I'd look at the grid and think, okay, why did they shift my lunch break by fifteen minutes? Why am I suddenly floating to the fourth floor on Thursday? I was absolutely convinced whoever was making the schedule was using it to send me coded messages, warning me that they were watching me or that I was in trouble. I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, trying to decode it. So, yeah, even when the depression took a break, that other stuff... it was still right there with me for weeks.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. [Name], it's... it's a lot of things, really, but I guess the main thing that finally made me make the appointment is my eating. It’s just gotten completely off track lately. I mean, some days I wake up and the thought of food is just... I don't know, it's almost exhausting. I’ll barely get down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before I head in for a shift. And working in healthcare support, you know, you're on your feet, you're moving patients, you're running around, so you need the energy. But I just can't seem to force myself to eat a real meal. And I’ve really started to notice it, too. Like, my scrubs, the ones I've worn for a couple of years now, they’re just hanging loose on me. I have to pull the drawstrings so tight just to keep the pants up. And it's not like I'm trying to lose weight or go on a diet or anything like that. I just... I don't have the appetite, and the weight is just falling off me without me even trying. It's been going on for a while now, and I just keep thinking it'll get better, that I'll wake up hungry one day, but it hasn't happened.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks, have you noticed a change in your mood, like feeling sad, down, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, I have. I don't even know if \"sad\" is the right word for it, really. It's more like... well, you know how I mentioned my mornings before, with the coffee and everything? I've been trying to figure out how to explain it to myself. Most mornings, my alarm goes off at 4:45 AM, because I usually like to have a little time to myself before the shift starts, just to ease into things. But lately, I wake up and I just lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling. \n\nI wake up with this... this incredibly heavy, almost hollow feeling right in the center of my chest. It's like the entire day is already completely ruined before I even get out from under the covers, you know? Before I even put on my hospital badge. I'll just look over at my lanyard sitting on the nightstand, and the thought of clipping it on and walking through those doors just feels impossible. \n\nI used to actually enjoy my morning routine—I'd watch the local news, check the weather, maybe read a bit on my phone. But now, all of that just feels so pointless. I have zero interest in the news or anything else I used to like. I just feel this dragging, empty weight, and it takes every single ounce of willpower I have just to put my feet on the floor. It's just this dark, hollow cloud that sits on me from the second I open my eyes, and I just keep wondering how I'm supposed to get through another twelve-hour shift feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Besides the appetite and mood changes, have you noticed any unexplained fevers, night sweats, or new lumps or swelling anywhere, like in your neck, armpits, or groin?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I check my temperature sometimes at work, you know, just out of habit because we have the thermometers right there at the nurses' station, the temporal ones that just scan across the forehead. And it's always normal, always right around 98.6 or maybe 97.9, depending on the time of day. I haven't had any fevers at all. And night sweats... well, sometimes I wake up feeling a little warm, but I think that's just because I sleep with a heavy comforter. I've always liked the weight of it, it helps me feel grounded, I guess. But I'm not waking up drenched or anything where I have to change my sheets. It's not like that. And as for lumps or swelling, no, I haven't noticed anything like that either. I mean, I shower every day, obviously, and I feel like I'd notice if there was something new or weird going on in my neck or under my arms. You hear stories, working in the hospital, you see patients come in with these swollen lymph nodes and it turns out to be something awful, so I'm pretty hyper-aware of my own body in that regard. But no, nothing like that. It's just... it's just the weight loss and this heavy feeling I was talking about. I keep checking my neck when I shave, just to be sure, but it's all completely normal.\n\nDoctor: When you notice your clothes fitting looser, how do you feel about that weight loss—are you happy or relieved about it, or does it worry you?\n\nPatient: Happy? Oh, no, definitely not happy. Honestly, it's... it's actually really unsettling. Like I was saying before, I'm not on a diet or anything. I know a lot of the folks I work with, especially some of the nurses on the floor, they're always talking about intermittent fasting or doing keto or whatever the new trend is, and they get so thrilled when they drop a few pounds. But for me, it just feels wrong. It really worries me, actually. \n\nI put on my jeans the other day—just my regular everyday Levi's that I always wear on my days off when I'm just running to the grocery store or the pharmacy—and I had to notch my belt two whole holes tighter. Two holes! And even then, the denim just looked all baggy and... I don't know, just sad on me. It really freaked me out for a minute. It worries me because it feels like my body is just shrinking, and I'm not doing anything to cause it on purpose, other than just not being able to stomach the thought of eating. It feels like my body is kind of failing me, or maybe I'm failing it, but I just don't have the energy to fix it. \n\nSo yeah, I'm definitely not relieved. If anything, it just adds to that whole heavy, dragging feeling I told you about, because now I'm lying there in bed staring at the ceiling, thinking about how my clothes don't fit and how I'm just sort of wasting away, and I still can't bring myself to even make a piece of toast. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors in your hands, a racing or pounding heartbeat, or feeling unusually hot or intolerant of warm environments recently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. When I'm helping transfer a patient from the bed to the chair, or adjusting their IV lines—not that I start the IVs, but I help make sure the tubing isn't tangled when we move them—I need to have a good grip. If my hands were shaking, I think I'd notice, and the nurses would definitely notice. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. \n\nAnd my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. If anything, it feels like everything in me is just moving in slow motion these days. Like I said about that heavy feeling in the mornings, it's almost like my heart is just barely thudding along, just doing the absolute bare minimum to keep me going. I don't get those sudden bursts where it feels like it's beating out of my chest. I know what that feels like—I had a panic attack once, years ago, back when I was still in school for my associate's degree, right before finals. My heart was going a mile a minute then, but I haven't had anything like that recently.\n\nAs for feeling hot... well, I mentioned the heavy comforter earlier, right? I'm usually the one who's a little chilly, if anything. The hospital is always kept freezing cold, especially on the surgical floors, so I'm used to wearing my long-sleeve undershirts beneath my scrubs. Even when I'm running around, doing a million things, answering call bells, I don't really break a sweat or feel overheated. I definitely don't feel intolerant to the heat. Honestly, a warm room sounds kind of nice right now, compared to how cold and empty everything else feels. So, no, no tremors, no racing heart, and definitely not feeling too hot. Just... just the same slow, heavy drag.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? No, no, I haven't made any kind of plan to do anything like that. I mean, working at the hospital, sometimes I get pulled from my regular duties to act as a 1:1 sitter for patients who are on suicide watch. I sit in the room with them for my whole twelve-hour shift, just watching them, making sure they're safe. I see what that kind of thing does to them, and I see the families when they come to visit, so I know the reality of it. Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. \n\nBut... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. \n\nIt just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: How long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? Like maybe I just caught a bug going around the hospital, or I was just stressed about some new protocol they rolled out on the floor. But no, it's been way, way longer than that. \n\nI actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. They made all the support staff park in the satellite lot, the one that's a good four blocks away, and we had to start taking this little shuttle bus in. \n\nI remember sitting on that shuttle bus one morning, looking out the window at the rain—it was a Tuesday, I think, and it was pouring—and just feeling this absolute, crushing weight drop onto me. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. \n\nBefore that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? That really locked in about a year and four months ago. \n\nSince then, it's just been every single day. Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things that others don't see, or having unusual beliefs—like feeling you're being punished or that something terrible is happening to your body—during this time?\n\nPatient: Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. \n\nSee, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. \n\nAnd then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. \n\nAnd you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. \n\nSo I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. Sometimes I'll just go into the clean supply closet and stand there staring at the stacks of extra blankets, just trying to catch my breath, because fighting the heavy feeling and the voices and the tracking all at once... it's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not feeling that heavy, depressed mood at all, but you still heard the voices or felt that people were tracking your charting?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually, there was a time like that. It’s funny you ask, because I remember thinking it was so strange. There was this period when that heavy, wet cement feeling I was telling you about actually eased up. It lifted enough that I felt almost like my old self. I even started running a little again. I used to jog around my neighborhood, just a couple of miles to clear my head, and I had completely stopped doing that when the hollow feeling set in. But for a little bit there, I was actually lacing up my sneakers, getting outside, and just getting through the day without feeling like I was dragging a thousand pounds behind me. I’d wake up and the alarm wouldn’t feel like a punishment. \n\nBut the weird thing is, the other stuff didn't stop. You'd think if my mood was better, my head would clear out entirely, right? But it didn't. For several weeks, even while I was feeling physically okay and getting through my shifts just fine, I was still dealing with it. I’d be walking down to the basement level to grab a bariatric wheelchair or something—totally empty hallway, nobody around at all, just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights—and I’d still hear those comments. Just whispering right there, criticizing me. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the voices, either. We have this big printed schedule they pin up on the corkboard in the breakroom, right by the time clock. Even when I was feeling good and running and eating normally, I kept reading that schedule over and over like it had hidden warnings about me. I'd look at the grid and think, okay, why did they shift my lunch break by fifteen minutes? Why am I suddenly floating to the fourth floor on Thursday? I was absolutely convinced whoever was making the schedule was using it to send me coded messages, warning me that they were watching me or that I was in trouble. I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, trying to decode it. So, yeah, even when the depression took a break, that other stuff... it was still right there with me for weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

F20.9 added as differential given prominent psychotic symptoms; further clarification on total duration of psychosis relative to mood episodes needed to distinguish between schizoaffective disorder and schizophrenia with mood symptoms.", "is_final": false}

healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? But no, it's been way, way longer than that. \n\nI actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. \n\nBefore that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things that others don't see, or having unusual beliefs—like feeling you're being punished or that something terrible is happening to your body—during this time?\n\nPatient: Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. \n\nSee, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. \n\nAnd then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. \n\nAnd you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. \n\nSo I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. It lifted enough that I felt almost like my old self. I even started running a little again. I used to jog around my neighborhood, just a couple of miles to clear my head, and I had completely stopped doing that when the hollow feeling set in. But for a little bit there, I was actually lacing up my sneakers, getting outside, and just getting through the day without feeling like I was dragging a thousand pounds behind me. I’d wake up and the alarm wouldn’t feel like a punishment. \n\nBut the weird thing is, the other stuff didn't stop. You'd think if my mood was better, my head would clear out entirely, right? But it didn't. For several weeks, even while I was feeling physically okay and getting through my shifts just fine, I was still dealing with it. I’d be walking down to the basement level to grab a bariatric wheelchair or something—totally empty hallway, nobody around at all, just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights—and I’d still hear those comments. Just whispering right there, criticizing me. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the voices, either. We have this big printed schedule they pin up on the corkboard in the breakroom, right by the time clock. Even when I was feeling good and running and eating normally, I kept reading that schedule over and over like it had hidden warnings about me. I'd look at the grid and think, okay, why did they shift my lunch break by fifteen minutes? Why am I suddenly floating to the fourth floor on Thursday? I was absolutely convinced whoever was making the schedule was using it to send me coded messages, warning me that they were watching me or that I was in trouble. I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, trying to decode it. So, yeah, even when the depression took a break, that other stuff... it was still right there with me for weeks.\n\n[Last Question]\nThat period when your mood improved and you were running again but still hearing the voices and feeling tracked—about how many weeks did that stretch last?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, let me think about that... how many weeks exactly. It’s hard to say precisely, because when you work in healthcare, time just sort of blurs together, you know? You’ve got your three twelves, or sometimes you pick up an extra shift because they’re short-staffed and they offer double time, and suddenly a week is gone and you don’t even know what day it is. But... if I really sit down and think about it, looking back at that specific time when I was lacing up my sneakers again and getting out there... 

I’d say it was a solid three, maybe four weeks. Yeah, about a month, give or take a few days. I remember because I had just bought a new pair of running shoes—these bright blue Asics, they have great arch support, which you really need when you're on your feet as much as I am—and I was breaking them in. And during that whole time, that whole three or four-week stretch, my mood was actually okay. Like I said, the heavy, dragging feeling was gone. I wasn't dreading the alarm clock. I was getting up, going for my run, making my coffee, and going into work feeling physically fine. 

But, like I was telling you, the other stuff just wouldn't quit. For that entire month, I’d be walking down to the basement level, completely alone in those empty hallways with just the hum of the fluorescent lights, and I’d still hear those voices making comments about me. Just whispering away. And the breakroom schedule... oh man, I spent that whole three or four weeks just staring at that corkboard. Every single day I clocked in, I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, absolutely convinced it was full of hidden warnings about me. So, yeah, I'd say it was a good three to four weeks where my mood had eased up, but I was still dealing with the voices and the schedule stuff every single day.

I’ll barely get down a cup of coffee and maybe a granola bar before I head in for a shift. It's just this dark, hollow cloud that sits on me from the second I open my eyes, and I just keep wondering how I'm supposed to get through another twelve-hour shift feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Besides the appetite and mood changes, have you noticed any unexplained fevers, night sweats, or new lumps or swelling anywhere, like in your neck, armpits, or groin?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I check my temperature sometimes at work, you know, just out of habit because we have the thermometers right there at the nurses' station, the temporal ones that just scan across the forehead. And it's always normal, always right around 98.6 or maybe 97.9, depending on the time of day. I haven't had any fevers at all. And night sweats... well, sometimes I wake up feeling a little warm, but I think that's just because I sleep with a heavy comforter. I've always liked the weight of it, it helps me feel grounded, I guess. But I'm not waking up drenched or anything where I have to change my sheets. It's not like that. And as for lumps or swelling, no, I haven't noticed anything like that either. I mean, I shower every day, obviously, and I feel like I'd notice if there was something new or weird going on in my neck or under my arms. You hear stories, working in the hospital, you see patients come in with these swollen lymph nodes and it turns out to be something awful, so I'm pretty hyper-aware of my own body in that regard. But no, nothing like that. It's just... it's just the weight loss and this heavy feeling I was talking about. I keep checking my neck when I shave, just to be sure, but it's all completely normal.\n\nDoctor: When you notice your clothes fitting looser, how do you feel about that weight loss—are you happy or relieved about it, or does it worry you?\n\nPatient: Happy? Oh, no, definitely not happy. Honestly, it's... it's actually really unsettling. Like I was saying before, I'm not on a diet or anything. I know a lot of the folks I work with, especially some of the nurses on the floor, they're always talking about intermittent fasting or doing keto or whatever the new trend is, and they get so thrilled when they drop a few pounds. But for me, it just feels wrong. It really worries me, actually. \n\nI put on my jeans the other day—just my regular everyday Levi's that I always wear on my days off when I'm just running to the grocery store or the pharmacy—and I had to notch my belt two whole holes tighter.
