It worries me because it feels like my body is just shrinking, and I'm not doing anything to cause it on purpose, other than just not being able to stomach the thought of eating. It feels like my body is kind of failing me, or maybe I'm failing it, but I just don't have the energy to fix it. \n\nSo yeah, I'm definitely not relieved. If anything, it just adds to that whole heavy, dragging feeling I told you about, because now I'm lying there in bed staring at the ceiling, thinking about how my clothes don't fit and how I'm just sort of wasting away, and I still can't bring myself to even make a piece of toast. It's just this constant, physical reminder every time I get dressed that something is really off track with me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any tremors in your hands, a racing or pounding heartbeat, or feeling unusually hot or intolerant of warm environments recently?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. I mean, my hands are pretty steady. They have to be, you know, for my job. When I'm helping transfer a patient from the bed to the chair, or adjusting their IV lines—not that I start the IVs, but I help make sure the tubing isn't tangled when we move them—I need to have a good grip. If my hands were shaking, I think I'd notice, and the nurses would definitely notice. Sometimes I'll hold my hand out in front of me just to check, just because I get anxious about things, but it's always steady. \n\nAnd my heart... no, it doesn't really race or pound. If anything, it feels like everything in me is just moving in slow motion these days. Like I said about that heavy feeling in the mornings, it's almost like my heart is just barely thudding along, just doing the absolute bare minimum to keep me going. I don't get those sudden bursts where it feels like it's beating out of my chest. I know what that feels like—I had a panic attack once, years ago, back when I was still in school for my associate's degree, right before finals. My heart was going a mile a minute then, but I haven't had anything like that recently.\n\nAs for feeling hot... well, I mentioned the heavy comforter earlier, right? I'm usually the one who's a little chilly, if anything. The hospital is always kept freezing cold, especially on the surgical floors, so I'm used to wearing my long-sleeve undershirts beneath my scrubs. Even when I'm running around, doing a million things, answering call bells, I don't really break a sweat or feel overheated. I definitely don't feel intolerant to the heat. Honestly, a warm room sounds kind of nice right now, compared to how cold and empty everything else feels. So, no, no tremors, no racing heart, and definitely not feeling too hot. Just... just the same slow, heavy drag.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of hurting yourself or wishing you weren't alive?\n\nPatient: Hurting myself? No, no, I haven't made any kind of plan to do anything like that. I mean, working at the hospital, sometimes I get pulled from my regular duties to act as a 1:1 sitter for patients who are on suicide watch. I sit in the room with them for my whole twelve-hour shift, just watching them, making sure they're safe. I see what that kind of thing does to them, and I see the families when they come to visit, so I know the reality of it. Because of that, no, I haven't sat down and tried to figure out a way to actually hurt myself. \n\nBut... the other part of your question, about wishing I wasn't alive... yeah. I do. I catch myself thinking that a lot lately, actually. It's usually at night when I'm lying in bed, like I was telling you before, under that heavy comforter. I'll just be lying there, staring up at the dark ceiling, maybe listening to the hum of the refrigerator in the kitchen or a car driving by outside, and I just start wishing that I just wouldn't wake up. \n\nIt just seems like it would be so much easier if I just... disappeared. Just faded away in my sleep. Because the thought of that alarm going off at 5:00 AM, and having to force myself to get up, and drag this heavy, tired body into the shower, put on my scrubs, and go pretend to be okay for a whole shift... it just feels like entirely too much. It's not that I want to do something violent to make it happen, I just... I get so tired of feeling like this dragging, empty shell, and I think, wouldn't it be a relief if I just wasn't here tomorrow? If the world just kept turning and I was just gone? It's a really unsettling thing to catch yourself thinking, but it just sneaks up on me when the apartment is quiet and I'm alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: How long has this heavy, hollow feeling and loss of interest been going on—would you say it's been more than two weeks now?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Oh, gosh, no. I wish it had only been two weeks. If it had only been two weeks, I think I could probably just brush it off as a bad patch, you know? Like maybe I just caught a bug going around the hospital, or I was just stressed about some new protocol they rolled out on the floor. But no, it's been way, way longer than that. \n\nI actually remember pretty clearly when this specific feeling—this heavy, hollow, dragging feeling where I just don't care about anything anymore—really settled in. It was... well, it's been roughly a year and four months now. I know that sounds really specific, but I remember it because it was right around the time they switched the parking assignments for the staff at the hospital. They made all the support staff park in the satellite lot, the one that's a good four blocks away, and we had to start taking this little shuttle bus in. \n\nI remember sitting on that shuttle bus one morning, looking out the window at the rain—it was a Tuesday, I think, and it was pouring—and just feeling this absolute, crushing weight drop onto me. It wasn't just being tired from the walk or annoyed about the parking. It was this deep, settling realization that I just didn't want to do any of it anymore. And it wasn't a passing thought. It just stayed. \n\nBefore that, things were... different. I mean, I had other things going on, other struggles that I'm sure we'll get into, but this steady, constant depression? This feeling like my battery is completely drained and won't recharge no matter how long I lie under that comforter? That really locked in about a year and four months ago. \n\nSince then, it's just been every single day. Every morning on that shuttle bus, every time I clock in, every time I go to the grocery store and just stare at the shelves because nothing sounds good. It's like someone turned down the volume and the color on my whole life, and they just left it that way for over a year. So yeah, definitely more than two weeks. It feels like it's been a lifetime, honestly, but if I look at the calendar, it's been about sixteen months of just... this.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, seeing things that others don't see, or having unusual beliefs—like feeling you're being punished or that something terrible is happening to your body—during this time?\n\nPatient: Voices... yeah. Yeah, I do hear things. And the other stuff you mentioned, about unusual beliefs... it's really hard to talk about, because I know how it sounds, especially working in healthcare, but... it's just so real to me. \n\nSee, at the hospital, we have to chart everything. Every single thing. Every time I take a set of vitals, every time I empty a Foley catheter, every time I help turn a patient every two hours so they don't get bedsores, I have to log into one of those computers on wheels—we call them COWs—and put it all into the system. But lately, I get this overwhelming feeling, this absolute certainty, that people are tracking my charting. Like they are watching every single keystroke I make, analyzing the timestamps, just waiting and watching me through the system. I don't even know exactly who is doing it, if it's administration or someone else entirely, but I can feel them tracking me. \n\nAnd then there are the voices. It's like this whispering, or sometimes it's louder, just right there in my ear when I'm standing in the hallway or even when I'm right in the middle of a patient's room. They say things about me, commenting on what I'm doing, telling me I'm messing up. \n\nAnd you'd think—at least, I would think—that with how completely drained I am from that heavy, awful depression I was just telling you about, maybe my brain would just be too tired to do all the other stuff. But it's not. That's the worst part of it all. When the depression is at its absolute worst, when I feel like I'm moving through wet cement and I'm just wishing I could disappear... the voices and the feeling that people are tracking my charting are still right there with it. \n\nSo I’m fighting both at the same time during shifts. I'm dragging this hollow, exhausted body down the fluorescent hallways, trying not to just collapse, and at the exact same time, I'm hearing these voices, and I'm absolutely terrified to type a patient's blood pressure into the computer because I know I'm being monitored. It's just exhausting. I mean, the physical work is hard enough, lifting people and being on your feet for twelve hours, but the mental gymnastics of trying to act completely normal while you're fighting this two-front war in your own head... it just takes everything out of you. Sometimes I'll just go into the clean supply closet and stand there staring at the stacks of extra blankets, just trying to catch my breath, because fighting the heavy feeling and the voices and the tracking all at once... it's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you were not feeling that heavy, depressed mood at all, but you still heard the voices or felt that people were tracking your charting?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, actually, there was a time like that. It’s funny you ask, because I remember thinking it was so strange. There was this period when that heavy, wet cement feeling I was telling you about actually eased up. It lifted enough that I felt almost like my old self. I even started running a little again. I used to jog around my neighborhood, just a couple of miles to clear my head, and I had completely stopped doing that when the hollow feeling set in. But for a little bit there, I was actually lacing up my sneakers, getting outside, and just getting through the day without feeling like I was dragging a thousand pounds behind me. I’d wake up and the alarm wouldn’t feel like a punishment. \n\nBut the weird thing is, the other stuff didn't stop. You'd think if my mood was better, my head would clear out entirely, right? But it didn't. For several weeks, even while I was feeling physically okay and getting through my shifts just fine, I was still dealing with it. I’d be walking down to the basement level to grab a bariatric wheelchair or something—totally empty hallway, nobody around at all, just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights—and I’d still hear those comments. Just whispering right there, criticizing me. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the voices, either. We have this big printed schedule they pin up on the corkboard in the breakroom, right by the time clock. Even when I was feeling good and running and eating normally, I kept reading that schedule over and over like it had hidden warnings about me. I'd look at the grid and think, okay, why did they shift my lunch break by fifteen minutes? Why am I suddenly floating to the fourth floor on Thursday? I was absolutely convinced whoever was making the schedule was using it to send me coded messages, warning me that they were watching me or that I was in trouble. I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, trying to decode it. So, yeah, even when the depression took a break, that other stuff... it was still right there with me for weeks.\n\nDoctor: That period when your mood improved and you were running again but still hearing the voices and feeling tracked—about how many weeks did that stretch last?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... how many weeks exactly. It’s hard to say precisely, because when you work in healthcare, time just sort of blurs together, you know? You’ve got your three twelves, or sometimes you pick up an extra shift because they’re short-staffed and they offer double time, and suddenly a week is gone and you don’t even know what day it is. But... if I really sit down and think about it, looking back at that specific time when I was lacing up my sneakers again and getting out there... \n\nI’d say it was a solid three, maybe four weeks. Yeah, about a month, give or take a few days. I remember because I had just bought a new pair of running shoes—these bright blue Asics, they have great arch support, which you really need when you're on your feet as much as I am—and I was breaking them in. And during that whole time, that whole three or four-week stretch, my mood was actually okay. Like I said, the heavy, dragging feeling was gone. I wasn't dreading the alarm clock. I was getting up, going for my run, making my coffee, and going into work feeling physically fine. \n\nBut, like I was telling you, the other stuff just wouldn't quit. For that entire month, I’d be walking down to the basement level, completely alone in those empty hallways with just the hum of the fluorescent lights, and I’d still hear those voices making comments about me. Just whispering away. And the breakroom schedule... oh man, I spent that whole three or four weeks just staring at that corkboard. Every single day I clocked in, I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, absolutely convinced it was full of hidden warnings about me. So, yeah, I'd say it was a good three to four weeks where my mood had eased up, but I was still dealing with the voices and the schedule stuff every single day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F25.1"], "note": "The patient confirmed a distinct ~3-4 week period of euthymic/improved mood (resumed running, no dragging fatigue) during which the auditory hallucinations and delusions of being tracked/coded messages persisted daily. This satisfies the schizoaffective criterion of psychotic symptoms occurring for a significant period (≥2 weeks) in the absence of mood symptoms, while the depressive episode has still been present for the vast majority (16 of ~16 months) of the illness course. This pattern is inconsistent with F20.9 (schizophrenia), which requires mood episodes to be brief relative to the total duration of psychotic illness, so schizophrenia is ruled out. The findings are most consistent with schizoaffective disorder, depressive type.", "is_final": true}

But it didn't. For several weeks, even while I was feeling physically okay and getting through my shifts just fine, I was still dealing with it. I’d be walking down to the basement level to grab a bariatric wheelchair or something—totally empty hallway, nobody around at all, just me and the hum of the fluorescent lights—and I’d still hear those comments. Just whispering right there, criticizing me. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the voices, either. We have this big printed schedule they pin up on the corkboard in the breakroom, right by the time clock. Even when I was feeling good and running and eating normally, I kept reading that schedule over and over like it had hidden warnings about me. I'd look at the grid and think, okay, why did they shift my lunch break by fifteen minutes? Why am I suddenly floating to the fourth floor on Thursday? I was absolutely convinced whoever was making the schedule was using it to send me coded messages, warning me that they were watching me or that I was in trouble. I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, trying to decode it. So, yeah, even when the depression took a break, that other stuff... it was still right there with me for weeks.\n\nDoctor: That period when your mood improved and you were running again but still hearing the voices and feeling tracked—about how many weeks did that stretch last?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... how many weeks exactly. It’s hard to say precisely, because when you work in healthcare, time just sort of blurs together, you know? You’ve got your three twelves, or sometimes you pick up an extra shift because they’re short-staffed and they offer double time, and suddenly a week is gone and you don’t even know what day it is. But... if I really sit down and think about it, looking back at that specific time when I was lacing up my sneakers again and getting out there... \n\nI’d say it was a solid three, maybe four weeks. Yeah, about a month, give or take a few days. I remember because I had just bought a new pair of running shoes—these bright blue Asics, they have great arch support, which you really need when you're on your feet as much as I am—and I was breaking them in. And during that whole time, that whole three or four-week stretch, my mood was actually okay. Like I said, the heavy, dragging feeling was gone. I wasn't dreading the alarm clock. I was getting up, going for my run, making my coffee, and going into work feeling physically fine. \n\nBut, like I was telling you, the other stuff just wouldn't quit. For that entire month, I’d be walking down to the basement level, completely alone in those empty hallways with just the hum of the fluorescent lights, and I’d still hear those voices making comments about me. Just whispering away. And the breakroom schedule... oh man, I spent that whole three or four weeks just staring at that corkboard. Every single day I clocked in, I’d stand there with my coffee, feeling totally fine energy-wise, but just obsessing over the highlighters and the shift assignments, absolutely convinced it was full of hidden warnings about me. So, yeah, I'd say it was a good three to four weeks where my mood had eased up, but I was still dealing with the voices and the schedule stuff every single day."
